<a id='question-126'></a>

### Question 126: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 126
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential injury patterns in the posterior thigh, specifically involving the sciatic nerve and its branches (tibial and common fibular nerves) in relation to the hamstring muscles and the popliteal fossa. (理解股后侧的解剖关系以及坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和总腓骨神经）与股二头肌、半腱样肌、半膜样肌的关系，以及它们在腘窝的潜在损伤模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male marathon runner presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden, severe pain in his right posterior thigh during a race. He reports feeling a "pop" followed by immediate weakness in his right leg, making it difficult to bear weight. On examination, his right knee is flexed at 90 degrees, and his right foot is plantarflexed and inverted (equinus and varus deformity). Sensory examination reveals numbness along the lateral aspect of his right lower leg and dorsum of his right foot. Palpation of the posterior thigh elicits tenderness over the region of the hamstring muscles. An MRI confirms a complete tear of the distal hamstring tendon complex (biceps femoris long head, semitendinosus, semimembranosus) with retraction of the muscle belly proximally. The MRI also shows evidence of compression of a major nerve bundle at the level of the injury. Which nerve is most likely compressed by the retracted hamstring muscle belly?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Superior gluteal nerve
(E) Femoral nerve

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male marathon runner (high activity level, potential for overuse injury).
- **Mechanism:** Sudden posterior thigh pain during running, "pop" sensation (suggests tendon/muscle tear).
- **Symptoms:** Immediate weakness, difficulty weight-bearing (significant motor deficit).
- **Physical Exam:** Right foot in equinus (plantarflexion) and varus (inversion) deformity. This specific foot posture strongly suggests dysfunction of the muscles responsible for dorsiflexion and eversion.
- **Sensory Exam:** Numbness on the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This sensory distribution corresponds to the territory of the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve, a branch of the common fibular nerve.
- **Imaging:** Complete tear of the distal hamstring tendon complex with proximal retraction of the muscle belly.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which nerve is compressed by the retracted hamstring muscle belly.

The combination of the equinus/varus foot deformity (loss of dorsiflexion and eversion) and sensory loss over the lateral leg/dorsum of the foot points directly to injury of the common fibular nerve. The retracted hamstring muscle belly, particularly the biceps femoris long head which originates high on the ischial tuberosity and runs laterally, is the most likely structure to compress the common fibular nerve as it courses around the fibular head.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Hamstring Muscles:** The distal hamstring tendons (biceps femoris long head, semitendinosus, semimembranosus) originate from the ischial tuberosity and insert distally on the tibia and fibula. The biceps femoris long head runs most laterally.
- **Sciatic Nerve:** The sciatic nerve originates from the sacral plexus (L4-S3) and exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle.
- **Nerve Division:** The sciatic nerve typically divides into the tibial nerve and the common fibular (peroneal) nerve in the posterior thigh, usually around the popliteal fossa or just proximal to it. The common fibular nerve courses laterally around the fibular head.
- **Common Fibular Nerve Course:** After dividing from the sciatic nerve, the common fibular nerve winds around the lateral aspect of the fibular head, passing superficial to the fibularis longus muscle. It then divides into the superficial fibular nerve (supplying the lateral compartment muscles and sensation to the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot) and the deep fibular nerve (supplying the anterior compartment muscles and sensation to the web space between the first and second toes).
- **Relationship:** The common fibular nerve is particularly vulnerable to injury as it wraps around the fibular head because it lies superficially. A retracted hamstring muscle belly, especially the biceps femoris long head, can impinge upon the sciatic nerve or its branches in the posterior thigh. Given the specific clinical presentation (equinus/varus foot, lateral leg/dorsum foot sensory loss), compression of the common fibular nerve is the most likely explanation. The tibial nerve supplies the posterior compartment muscles and sensation to the sole of the foot; injury would cause weakness in plantarflexion and sensory loss on the sole. The obturator nerve supplies the medial thigh compartment; the superior gluteal nerve supplies the gluteus medius, minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. The femoral nerve supplies the anterior thigh compartment and sensation to the anterior leg and medial foot. None of these match the patient's presentation.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The hamstring muscles develop from the posterior compartment of the hindlimb bud, derived from the somites.
- The sciatic nerve arises from the lumbosacral plexus, formed by ventral rami of spinal nerves L4-S3.
- The common fibular nerve is a branch of the sciatic nerve.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** Injury to the tibial nerve would cause weakness in plantarflexion (foot pointing down) and inversion, and sensory loss on the sole of the foot. The patient has equinus (plantarflexion) due to unopposed action of the gastrocnemius/soleus, but also varus (inversion) which is less typical for isolated tibial nerve injury, and the sensory loss is on the lateral leg/dorsum, not the sole. The hamstring tear is less likely to directly compress the tibial nerve, which runs more medially.
- (C) **Obturator nerve:** This nerve supplies the medial thigh muscles (adductors) and sensation to the medial thigh. Injury would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss in the medial thigh. This is inconsistent with the patient's lower leg/foot symptoms. The obturator nerve passes through the obturator foramen and is not in the posterior thigh region vulnerable to hamstring injury.
- (D) **Superior gluteal nerve:** This nerve supplies the gluteus medius, minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and stabilization, leading to a Trendelenburg gait. It is not related to the patient's lower leg/foot symptoms. The superior gluteal nerve exits the pelvis superior to the piriformis muscle.
- (E) **Femoral nerve:** This nerve supplies the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps, sartorius, pectineus) and sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg/foot. Injury would cause weakness in knee extension and hip flexion, and sensory loss in the anterior thigh and medial leg/foot. This is inconsistent with the patient's posterior thigh injury and lower leg/foot symptoms. The femoral nerve passes under the inguinal ligament and is located in the anterior thigh.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Common Fibular Nerve (Correct) | Tibial Nerve (Distractor A) | Obturator Nerve (Distractor C) | Superior Gluteal Nerve (Distractor D) | Femoral Nerve (Distractor E) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------- |
| **Location of Injury**   | Around fibular head, posterior thigh | Posterior thigh, popliteal fossa | Pelvis/Medial Thigh            | Pelvis/Gluteal Region                 | Anterior Thigh              |
| **Muscles Supplied**    | Lateral & Anterior Leg         | Posterior Leg               | Medial Thigh                   | Gluteus Medius/Minimus/TFL            | Anterior Thigh              |
| **Motor Deficit**       | Foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness), Eversion weakness | Plantarflexion weakness, Inversion weakness | Thigh adduction weakness             | Hip abduction weakness          | Knee extension weakness     |
| **Sensory Deficit**     | Lateral Leg, Dorsum Foot       | Sole of Foot                | Medial Thigh                   | Lateral Hip/Thigh                    | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg   |
| **Foot Posture**        | Equinus, Varus (Foot Drop)      | Equinus                    | N/A                            | N/A                                   | N/A                         |
| **Hamstring Relation** | Vulnerable to retraction       | Less vulnerable             | Not related                    | Not related                           | Not related                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular (peroneal) nerve is vulnerable to injury as it wraps around the fibular head. Compression or traction injuries in the posterior thigh or at the fibular head can occur, often associated with hamstring injuries or knee dislocations. The classic presentation of common fibular nerve injury is foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexion and eversion) and sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot, resulting in an equinus and varus foot posture. Differentiating common fibular nerve injury from tibial nerve injury is crucial based on the specific motor and sensory deficits and foot posture.

<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 127
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential vulnerabilities of the sciatic nerve and its branches (tibial and common peroneal/fibular) within the posterior thigh and leg, specifically concerning the popliteal fossa and the fibular head, and differentiating clinical presentations based on specific nerve injury patterns. (理解坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和腓神经）在股后部和腿部的解剖关系及其潜在脆弱性，特别是关于腘窝和腓骨头，并区分基于特定神经损伤模式的临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a significant blunt trauma to his left posterior knee during a motor vehicle accident. He complains of severe pain in his left lower leg and foot, accompanied by numbness and weakness. On physical examination, his left leg is held in a slightly flexed position at the knee, and the foot is inverted and plantarflexed (equinus deformity). He is unable to dorsiflex his foot or evert it against resistance. Sensory examination reveals numbness over the dorsum of the foot and the lateral aspect of the lower leg, but sensation is intact on the plantar surface of the foot and the medial aspect of the lower leg. Palpation reveals tenderness over the lateral aspect of the left knee, specifically around the fibular head. Ankle reflexes (Achilles and plantar) are absent on the left side. Patellar reflex is normal bilaterally. Straight leg raise test is negative.

(A) Injury to the tibial nerve proximal to its bifurcation.
(B) Injury to the common peroneal (fibular) nerve at the fibular head.
(C) Injury to the femoral nerve in the anterior thigh.
(D) Injury to the obturator nerve in the medial thigh.
(E) Injury to the superior gluteal nerve in the buttock.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Blunt trauma to the posterior knee. This points towards potential injury to structures passing through or near the popliteal fossa or around the knee joint.
- **Symptoms:** Severe pain, numbness, weakness in the left lower leg and foot. This indicates nerve injury.
- **Physical Exam - Position:** Leg flexed at the knee, foot inverted and plantarflexed (equinus deformity). This specific posture is characteristic of common peroneal nerve injury, as the muscles responsible for dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus - innervated by deep peroneal nerve) and eversion (peroneus longus and brevis - innervated by superficial peroneal nerve) are paralyzed, leading to unopposed action of the plantarflexors (gastrocnemius, soleus - innervated by tibial nerve) and invertors (tibialis posterior - innervated by tibial nerve).
- **Physical Exam - Motor:** Inability to dorsiflex or evert the foot. This directly implicates the common peroneal nerve, as it innervates all muscles responsible for these actions (deep and superficial peroneal branches).
- **Physical Exam - Sensory:** Numbness over the dorsum of the foot and lateral lower leg. This sensory distribution corresponds to the common peroneal nerve (superficial and deep branches). Sensation intact on the plantar surface and medial lower leg corresponds to the tibial nerve distribution.
- **Physical Exam - Reflexes:** Absent ankle (Achilles) reflex. The Achilles reflex tests the tibial nerve (S1-S2). Absent plantar reflex is less specific but can be affected by lower motor neuron lesions. Normal patellar reflex (L2-L4, femoral nerve) rules out significant femoral nerve injury.
- **Palpation:** Tenderness over the lateral aspect of the knee, specifically around the fibular head. The common peroneal nerve is particularly vulnerable as it wraps around the fibular head.

All these findings strongly point to an injury affecting the common peroneal nerve, specifically at or near the fibular head, given the mechanism and location of tenderness.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve:** A branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S3). It arises in the posterior thigh, typically at the level of the popliteal fossa, posterior to the knee joint.
- **Course:** It descends laterally, passing superficial to the lateral head of the gastrocnemius muscle. It then winds around the lateral aspect of the fibular head, entering the anterior compartment of the leg.
- **Branches:** Just proximal to or at the fibular head, it divides into the superficial peroneal (fibular) nerve and the deep peroneal (fibular) nerve.
    - **Superficial Peroneal Nerve:** Innervates the peroneus longus and brevis muscles (eversion) and provides sensory innervation to the lateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (except the first web space).
    - **Deep Peroneal Nerve:** Innervates the tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, and peroneus tertius muscles (dorsiflexion and toe extension) and provides sensory innervation to the first web space between the great toe and second toe.
- **Vulnerability:** The nerve's superficial course around the fibular head makes it highly susceptible to injury from direct trauma, compression, or fractures in this region.
- **Clinical Presentation of Injury at Fibular Head:** Loss of dorsiflexion (foot drop), loss of eversion, sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot, and an equinus deformity (plantarflexion) due to unopposed action of the plantarflexors. This matches the patient's presentation perfectly.
- **Tibial Nerve:** The other major branch of the sciatic nerve. It runs down the posterior leg, deep to the soleus muscle. It innervates the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexion, inversion) and provides sensory innervation to the plantar surface of the foot and the posterior aspect of the lower leg. Injury would cause weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, sensory loss on the sole, and loss of the Achilles reflex, but *not* foot drop or loss of dorsiflexion/eversion.
- **Femoral Nerve:** Innervates the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensory innervation to the anterior thigh and medial leg (via the saphenous nerve). Injury causes quadriceps weakness (difficulty extending the knee), sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg, and loss of the patellar reflex.
- **Obturator Nerve:** Innervates the medial thigh muscles (adductors) and provides sensory innervation to the medial thigh. Injury causes weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss in the medial thigh.
- **Superior Gluteal Nerve:** Innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae muscles. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and stabilization, leading to a Trendelenburg gait.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The sciatic nerve, and its branches including the common peroneal and tibial nerves, arise from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3 nerve roots). The common peroneal nerve specifically receives contributions from L4, L5, S1, and S2. The tibial nerve receives contributions from L4, L5, S1, S2, and S3. The femoral nerve arises from L2, L3, and L4. The obturator nerve arises from L2, L3, and L4. The superior gluteal nerve arises from L4, L5, and S1.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Injury to the tibial nerve proximal to its bifurcation. This would cause weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, sensory loss on the sole of the foot and posterior leg, and loss of the Achilles reflex. It would *not* cause foot drop (inability to dorsiflex) or loss of eversion. The patient's presentation is inconsistent with isolated tibial nerve injury.
- (C) Injury to the femoral nerve in the anterior thigh. This would cause weakness in knee extension (quadriceps paralysis), sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg, and loss of the patellar reflex. The patient has a normal patellar reflex and weakness/sensory loss in the lower leg/foot, not the thigh.
- (D) Injury to the obturator nerve in the medial thigh. This would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. This is inconsistent with the patient's lower leg and foot deficits.
- (E) Injury to the superior gluteal nerve in the buttock. This would cause weakness in hip abduction (gluteus medius/minimus) and potentially external rotation (tensor fasciae latae), leading to a Trendelenburg gait. This is inconsistent with the patient's lower leg and foot deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Common Peroneal Nerve Injury (Correct) | Tibial Nerve Injury        | Femoral Nerve Injury       | Obturator Nerve Injury     | Superior Gluteal Nerve Injury |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Location of Injury**  | Fibular Head (Posterior Knee)          | Posterior Leg              | Anterior Thigh             | Medial Thigh               | Buttock                        |
| **Motor Deficit**       | Foot Drop (no dorsiflexion/eversion)   | Weak Plantarflexion/Inversion | Weak Knee Extension        | Weak Thigh Adduction       | Weak Hip Abduction             |
| **Sensory Deficit**     | Lateral Leg, Dorsum of Foot           | Plantar Foot, Posterior Leg | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg | Medial Thigh               | Lateral Buttock                 |
| **Reflex Affected**     | None (Achilles absent if tibial also affected, but primary deficit is motor/sensory) | Absent Achilles Reflex     | Absent Patellar Reflex     | None                         | None                           |
| **Foot Position**       | Equinus (Plantarflexed), Inverted      | Plantarflexed              | Normal (if isolated)       | Normal (if isolated)       | Normal (if isolated)           |
| **Key Muscles Affected**| Tibialis Anterior, EDL, EDL, Peroneus Longus/Brevis | Gastrocnemius, Soleus, Tibialis Posterior | Quadriceps Femoris, Sartorius | Adductor Magnus, Longus, Brevis | Gluteus Medius, Minimus        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of foot drop (inability to dorsiflex), sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg, and an equinus deformity following trauma to the posterior knee is classic for common peroneal nerve injury, particularly at the fibular head where it is most vulnerable. Differentiating this from tibial nerve injury (plantarflexion weakness, sole sensory loss) or femoral nerve injury (quadriceps weakness, anterior thigh sensory loss) is crucial. Remember the "foot drop" sign points strongly towards peroneal nerve involvement.

<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 128
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the gluteal region, specifically the course of the sciatic nerve relative to the piriformis muscle, and the clinical implications of sciatic nerve injury or compression. (理解臀区解剖关系，特别是坐骨神经相对于梨状肌的走行，以及坐骨神经损伤或压迫的临床意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe right lower back and buttock pain radiating down the posterior aspect of his right leg, extending to the lateral foot. The pain began acutely 3 days ago after receiving an intramuscular injection in the right buttock for a severe upper respiratory infection. On physical examination, the patient exhibits significant tenderness to palpation over the right gluteal region. Motor examination reveals weakness in plantarflexion (medical research council grade 3/5) and dorsiflexion (MRC grade 4/5) of the right foot. Sensory examination shows decreased sensation along the lateral aspect of the right lower leg and the dorsum of the right foot. The Achilles reflex (S1) is diminished on the right side. Straight leg raise test is positive at 30 degrees on the right. An MRI of the lumbar spine and pelvis reveals no evidence of disc herniation or spinal stenosis. However, the MRI shows evidence of edema and inflammation surrounding the piriformis muscle, with apparent compression of a major nerve bundle exiting the pelvis.

Which nerve is most likely compressed in this patient?

(A) Pudendal nerve
(B) Superior gluteal nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Sciatic nerve
(E) Femoral nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **History:** Acute onset of right buttock pain radiating down the posterior leg to the lateral foot after an intramuscular injection in the buttock. This suggests injury to a structure in the gluteal region, potentially related to the injection site.
- **Physical Exam:**
    - Tenderness over the right gluteal region: Localizes the pathology to the buttock.
    - Weakness in plantarflexion (gastrocnemius/soleus, S1-S2) and dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, L4-L5, deep fibular nerve branch of common fibular nerve, S1-S2). This indicates involvement of multiple muscles innervated by different parts of the sciatic nerve.
    - Decreased sensation along the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot: Corresponds to the distribution of the common fibular (peroneal) nerve (S1-S2), a major branch of the sciatic nerve.
    - Diminished Achilles reflex (S1): Indicates involvement of the S1 nerve root or the tibial nerve (S1-S2), a major branch of the sciatic nerve.
    - Positive straight leg raise test: Suggests nerve root irritation or sciatic nerve irritation, consistent with the radiating pain pattern.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows no lumbar spine pathology but edema/inflammation around the piriformis muscle compressing a major nerve bundle. This strongly points to piriformis syndrome or direct injury to the nerve passing near or through the piriformis muscle.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of buttock pain radiating down the posterior leg, specific motor deficits (plantarflexion, dorsiflexion), sensory loss in the lateral leg/dorsum of foot, diminished Achilles reflex, and MRI findings localizing compression near the piriformis muscle strongly implicates the sciatic nerve.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve (坐骨神经):** The largest nerve in the body, formed in the pelvis by the union of the dorsal divisions of the L4, L5, S1, S2, and S3 nerve roots. It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle.
- **Piriformis Muscle (梨状肌):** Located in the deep gluteal region, it originates from the anterior surface of the sacrum and inserts onto the greater trochanter of the femur. It externally rotates the hip.
- **Relationship:** The sciatic nerve typically exits the pelvis *inferior* to the piriformis muscle. However, anatomical variations exist (~10-15% of individuals) where the sciatic nerve splits into tibial and common fibular divisions *before* passing through or under the piriformis muscle, or the nerve itself passes *through* the muscle.
- **Piriformis Syndrome (梨状肌综合征):** A condition where the piriformis muscle compresses or irritates the sciatic nerve, causing buttock pain radiating down the leg (sciatica). This can be caused by muscle spasm, hypertrophy, trauma, or anatomical variations. Intramuscular injections in the gluteal region can also cause direct injury or inflammation leading to compression.
- **Clinical Presentation:** The patient's symptoms (pain distribution, motor weakness in plantarflexion and dorsiflexion, sensory loss in lateral leg/dorsum of foot, diminished Achilles reflex) are classic for sciatic nerve involvement affecting both its tibial (plantarflexion, sensation medial foot/sole, Achilles reflex) and common fibular (dorsiflexion, sensation lateral leg/dorsum of foot) divisions.
- **Injection Site:** Intramuscular injections in the upper outer quadrant of the buttock are recommended to avoid sciatic nerve injury. However, injections given too low or deep can injure the nerve or cause inflammation/hematoma leading to compression, especially near the piriformis muscle.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Pudendal nerve (耻骨神经):** Arises from S2-S4. It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, loops around the ischial spine, and re-enters the pelvis through the lesser sciatic foramen to supply the perineum. Injury typically causes perineal numbness (saddle anesthesia) and bowel/bladder dysfunction. This does not match the patient's symptoms.
- (B) **Superior gluteal nerve (上臀神经):** Arises from L4-S1. It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, superior to the piriformis muscle. It innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. This does not match the patient's symptoms.
- (C) **Obturator nerve (闭孔神经):** Arises from L2-L4. It exits the pelvis through the obturator foramen. It innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Injury causes weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss in the medial thigh. This does not match the patient's symptoms.
- (E) **Femoral nerve (股神经):** Arises from L2-L4. It exits the abdomen via the psoas major muscle, passes under the inguinal ligament, and enters the thigh. It innervates the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg/foot (via the saphenous nerve). Injury causes weakness in knee extension and hip flexion, and sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg. This does not match the patient's symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Sciatic Nerve (坐骨神经) | Pudendal Nerve (耻骨神经) | Superior Gluteal Nerve (上臀神经) | Obturator Nerve (闭孔神经) | Femoral Nerve (股神经) |
|----------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------------|------------------------|
| **Origin**           | L4-S3                    | S2-S4                     | L4-S1                            | L2-L4                      | L2-L4                  |
| **Exit Pelvis**      | Greater Sciatic Foramen  | Greater Sciatic Foramen   | Greater Sciatic Foramen         | Obturator Foramen         | Psoas Major Muscle     |
| **Key Relation**     | Piriformis Muscle        | Ischial Spine             | Superior to Piriformis          | Obturator Membrane         | Inguinal Ligament      |
| **Innervation**      | Posterior Thigh, Leg, Foot| Perineum                  | Gluteus Medius/Minimus, TFL     | Medial Thigh Adductors    | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg|
| **Motor Deficit**    | Plantarflexion/Dorsiflexion Weakness, Foot Drop | Perineal/Anal Sphincter Weakness | Hip Abduction Weakness     | Thigh Adduction Weakness | Knee Extension Weakness|
| **Sensory Deficit**  | Posterior Leg/Foot, Lateral Leg/Dorsum Foot | Saddle Anesthesia             | Lateral Hip/Thigh            | Medial Thigh              | Anterior Thigh/Medial Leg|
| **Clinical Scenario**| Sciatica, Foot Drop      | Perineal Numbness, Bowel/Bladder Issues | Trendelenburg Gait           | Medial Thigh Numbness     | Quadriceps Weakness    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The sciatic nerve is vulnerable to compression or injury in the gluteal region, particularly near the piriformis muscle (piriformis syndrome) or due to intramuscular injections. Its extensive distribution explains the wide range of motor and sensory deficits seen in sciatic neuropathy. Differentiating sciatic nerve injury from other lower limb neuropathies requires careful localization based on the specific muscles affected and the sensory distribution. Key differentials include lesions of the lumbar plexus (femoral, obturator nerves) or sacral plexus (pudendal, superior gluteal nerves), which have distinct anatomical courses and clinical presentations.

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 129
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the posterior thigh, specifically the sciatic nerve and its divisions, and the structures at risk during intramuscular injections in the gluteal region. (理解大腿后侧的解剖关系，特别是坐骨神经及其分支，以及臀部肌肉内注射时受损的结构。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old female presents to the emergency department after receiving an intramuscular injection of ceftriaxone in her right buttock at an urgent care clinic for suspected cellulitis. The injection was administered by a nurse practitioner. Shortly after the injection, the patient reported immediate, severe pain radiating down the posterior aspect of her right leg, followed by numbness and weakness in her right foot and ankle. On physical examination, she exhibits foot drop (inability to dorsiflex the foot) and sensory loss over the lateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot. The deep tendon reflexes at the Achilles tendon (S1) and patellar tendon (L3/L4) are intact, but the plantar reflex (S1) is absent on the right side. An MRI of the right hip and thigh reveals inflammation and edema surrounding the sciatic nerve within the gluteus maximus muscle. Which structure was most likely injured by the injection?

(A) Superior gluteal nerve
(B) Pudendal nerve
(C) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve
(D) Tibial nerve
(E) Obturator nerve

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old female, right buttock injection, immediate severe pain radiating down posterior leg, numbness, weakness in foot/ankle. This points to nerve injury in the gluteal region affecting the lower limb.
- **Physical Exam:** Foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness) indicates common peroneal nerve injury. Sensory loss over lateral lower leg and dorsum of foot also points to common peroneal nerve distribution. Intact Achilles reflex (S1) and patellar reflex (L3/L4) suggest the injury is distal to the sciatic nerve bifurcation or involves only the common peroneal division. Absent plantar reflex (S1) is less specific but consistent with lower motor neuron involvement affecting S1.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows inflammation around the sciatic nerve in the gluteus maximus. This confirms a sciatic nerve injury in the gluteal region.
- **Injection Site:** Intramuscular injection in the buttock is the inciting event.
- **Question:** Asks for the *specific* structure injured.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve:** The largest nerve in the body, originating from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3 roots). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle.
- **Course in Gluteal Region:** It runs deep to the gluteus maximus muscle in the posterior thigh.
- **Bifurcation:** Typically bifurcates into the tibial nerve and the common peroneal (fibular) nerve in the posterior thigh, often near the popliteal fossa, but sometimes higher up, even within the gluteal region.
- **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve:** Supplies muscles for dorsiflexion and eversion of the foot, and provides sensation to the anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. Injury leads to foot drop and sensory loss in its distribution.
- **Tibial Nerve:** Supplies muscles for plantarflexion and inversion of the foot, and provides sensation to the sole of the foot.
- **Gluteal Injections:** Intramuscular injections in the gluteal region are typically given in the upper outer quadrant of the buttock (ventrogluteal site) to avoid injury to the sciatic nerve and its branches. However, injections given too low or too medial can injure the sciatic nerve or its divisions.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (foot drop, sensory loss over lateral leg/dorsum of foot) are classic signs of common peroneal nerve injury. The location of the injury (gluteal region, post-injection) strongly suggests the sciatic nerve or one of its divisions was damaged. Given the specific deficits, the common peroneal nerve is the most likely structure injured. The MRI finding supports sciatic nerve involvement in the gluteus maximus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior gluteal nerve:** Innervates gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. It exits the pelvis superior to the piriformis muscle, well above the typical injection site and sciatic nerve course. This nerve injury does not cause foot drop or sensory loss in the lower leg/foot.
- (B) **Pudendal nerve:** Arises from S2-S4. Exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis, then loops around the ischial spine to enter the perineum through the lesser sciatic foramen. It provides motor innervation to perineal muscles and sensation to the perineum and external genitalia. Injury causes saddle anesthesia and bowel/bladder dysfunction. It is not located in the posterior thigh and is not typically injured by gluteal injections.
- (D) **Tibial nerve:** A division of the sciatic nerve. Injury causes weakness in plantarflexion and inversion of the foot, and sensory loss on the sole of the foot. While the sciatic nerve itself could be injured, the specific symptoms (foot drop) point to the common peroneal division. If the *entire* sciatic nerve were injured, both common peroneal and tibial nerve deficits would be expected.
- (E) **Obturator nerve:** Arises from L2-L4. Exits the pelvis through the obturator foramen. Innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Injury causes weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. It is located in the anterior/medial thigh, not the posterior thigh or gluteal region, and is not related to sciatic nerve injury or gluteal injections.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Origin        | Course                                  | Innervation/Sensation                                                                 | Injury Symptoms                                                                                                | Relevance to Vignette                                                                                                |
| :------------------------ | :------------ | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve** | L4-S2 (Sciatic) | Sciatic nerve -> posterior thigh -> bifurcates -> wraps around fibular neck | Lateral compartment muscles (dorsiflexion, eversion); sensation to anterolateral leg & dorsum of foot | Foot drop, sensory loss lateral leg/dorsum foot                                                                 | **Matches patient's symptoms (foot drop, sensory loss). Likely injured by gluteal injection affecting sciatic nerve.** |
| Superior Gluteal Nerve    | L4-S1          | Exits pelvis superior to piriformis      | Gluteus medius, minimus, TFL (hip abduction)                                          | Weak hip abduction, Trendelenburg gait                                                                          | Incorrect location, different function, different symptoms.                                                              |
| Pudendal Nerve            | S2-S4          | Exits pelvis via greater sciatic foramen, loops ischial spine | Perineal muscles, sensation to perineum/genitalia                                                              | Saddle anesthesia, bowel/bladder dysfunction                                                                    | Incorrect location, different function, different symptoms.                                                              |
| Tibial Nerve              | L4-S3 (Sciatic) | Sciatic nerve -> posterior thigh -> bifurcates -> popliteal fossa | Posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexion, inversion); sensation to sole of foot | Weak plantarflexion/inversion, sensory loss sole of foot                                                          | Injury would cause different symptoms (plantarflexion weakness).                                                         |
| Obturator Nerve           | L2-L4          | Exits pelvis via obturator foramen       | Medial compartment muscles (adduction); sensation to medial thigh                     | Weak thigh adduction, sensory loss medial thigh                                                                 | Incorrect location, different function, different symptoms.                                                              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Intramuscular injections in the gluteal region carry a risk of sciatic nerve injury, particularly if administered improperly (too low, too medial). Injury to the common peroneal division of the sciatic nerve results in foot drop and sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot. Differentiating common peroneal nerve injury from tibial nerve injury or other lower limb neuropathies relies on understanding their specific motor and sensory distributions. Always consider the location of the injury relative to the anatomical course of the nerves.

<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 130
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential injury patterns in the posterior thigh, specifically involving the sciatic nerve and its branches (tibial and common peroneal/fibular nerves) in relation to the hamstring muscles and the popliteal fossa. (理解股后侧的解剖关系以及坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和腓神经）与股后肌群和膝窝的关系，以及潜在的损伤模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male professional soccer player is brought to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to the posterior aspect of his left thigh during a match. He reports immediate, severe pain and inability to move his left foot dorsiflex or plantarflex. On physical examination, his left leg is in a flexed position at the hip and knee. Palpation reveals significant tenderness and swelling over the posterior thigh, approximately 10 cm proximal to the popliteal fossa. Neurological examination shows absent ankle reflexes (Achilles and plantar) on the left side. Motor examination reveals complete paralysis of plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus) and dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus) of the left foot. Sensory examination shows loss of sensation over the lateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot. The medial aspect of the lower leg and the sole of the foot retain normal sensation. An MRI confirms a complete transection of a major nerve in the posterior thigh.

Which nerve is most likely transected?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Superior gluteal nerve
(E) Femoral nerve

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Direct blow to the posterior thigh. This localizes the injury to the structures in that region.
- **Symptoms:** Immediate severe pain, inability to move the foot (both dorsiflexion and plantarflexion). This indicates a major motor nerve injury affecting the entire lower leg and foot.
- **Physical Exam:** Tenderness and swelling 10 cm proximal to the popliteal fossa. This pinpoints the location of the injury in the posterior thigh, above the knee joint.
- **Neurological Exam:**
    - Absent ankle reflexes (Achilles and plantar). Achilles reflex tests the S1 nerve root via the tibial nerve; plantar reflex tests the S1-S2 nerve roots via the tibial nerve. Absence indicates tibial nerve dysfunction.
    - Complete paralysis of plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus). These muscles are innervated by the tibial nerve.
    - Complete paralysis of dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus). These muscles are innervated by the common peroneal (fibular) nerve.
    - Sensory loss over the lateral aspect of the lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This is the distribution of the common peroneal (fibular) nerve (specifically, the superficial and deep peroneal nerves).
    - Normal sensation over the medial aspect of the lower leg and sole of the foot. This is the distribution of the tibial nerve.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms complete transection of a major nerve in the posterior thigh.
- **Synthesis:** The injury is in the posterior thigh. The patient has complete loss of function (motor and sensory) in the entire lower leg and foot, affecting both plantarflexion (tibial nerve) and dorsiflexion (common peroneal nerve). The key is that the injury occurred *proximal* to the bifurcation of the sciatic nerve into the tibial and common peroneal nerves. A lesion affecting only the tibial nerve would spare dorsiflexion and the lateral leg/dorsum sensation. A lesion affecting only the common peroneal nerve would spare plantarflexion and the medial leg/sole sensation. A lesion affecting the sciatic nerve *before* its bifurcation would affect *both* branches, leading to the observed combined deficits. The location (posterior thigh, proximal to popliteal fossa) is consistent with the sciatic nerve.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve:** The sciatic nerve is the largest nerve in the body, formed in the upper posterior thigh by the union of the tibial and common peroneal (fibular) nerves, which arise from the sacral plexus (L4-S3 roots). It descends deep to the gluteus maximus muscle, through the greater sciatic foramen, and then runs down the posterior thigh, deep to the hamstring muscles (biceps femoris, semitendinosus, semimembranosus).
- **Bifurcation:** Typically, the sciatic nerve bifurcates into the tibial and common peroneal nerves in the posterior thigh, usually about 10 cm proximal to the medial epicondyle of the femur (popliteal fossa).
- **Tibial Nerve:** The tibial nerve is the larger branch. It runs down the posterior leg, deep to the soleus muscle, and enters the foot between the medial malleolus and the calcaneal tendon. It innervates the posterior compartment muscles (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus) and provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot and the medial aspect of the lower leg.
- **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve:** This nerve runs laterally around the neck of the fibula. It divides into the superficial peroneal nerve (innervates lateral compartment muscles - peroneus longus, peroneus brevis - and provides sensation to the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot) and the deep peroneal nerve (innervates anterior compartment muscles - tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus, peroneus tertius - and provides sensation to the web space between the first and second toes).
- **Clinical Correlation:** A lesion in the posterior thigh, proximal to the bifurcation, affects both the tibial and common peroneal nerves, resulting in paralysis of both plantarflexion (tibial) and dorsiflexion (common peroneal), along with corresponding sensory loss. The location described (posterior thigh, 10 cm proximal to popliteal fossa) is the classic site for sciatic nerve injury or bifurcation injury. The patient's symptoms perfectly match a complete sciatic nerve lesion at this level.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** Injury to the tibial nerve *after* the sciatic bifurcation (e.g., in the popliteal fossa or posterior leg) would cause paralysis of plantarflexion and sensory loss on the sole/medial leg, but *dorsiflexion* would be spared, and sensation on the lateral leg/dorsum would be intact. This contradicts the patient's presentation.
- (B) **Common peroneal (fibular) nerve:** Injury to the common peroneal nerve (e.g., around the fibular neck) would cause paralysis of dorsiflexion and sensory loss on the lateral leg/dorsum, but *plantarflexion* would be spared, and sensation on the sole/medial leg would be intact. This contradicts the patient's presentation.
- (C) **Obturator nerve:** The obturator nerve arises from the lumbar plexus (L2-L4) and passes through the obturator foramen to innervate the medial compartment muscles of the thigh (adductors). Injury causes weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. This is completely unrelated to the posterior thigh injury and lower leg/foot deficits.
- (D) **Superior gluteal nerve:** The superior gluteal nerve arises from the sacral plexus (L4-S1) and innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae muscles. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. This is unrelated to the posterior thigh injury and lower leg/foot deficits.
- (E) **Femoral nerve:** The femoral nerve arises from the lumbar plexus (L2-L4) and passes under the inguinal ligament to innervate the anterior compartment muscles of the thigh (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensory innervation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury causes weakness in knee extension and hip flexion, and sensory loss in the anterior thigh and medial leg. This is unrelated to the posterior thigh injury and lower leg/foot deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location of Injury | Motor Deficits                                  | Sensory Deficits                               | Reflexes Affected |
|----------------------|--------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| **Sciatic Nerve (Proximal to Bifurcation)** | Posterior Thigh (proximal to popliteal fossa) | Paralysis of plantarflexion AND dorsiflexion | Loss of sensation on sole/medial leg AND lateral leg/dorsum | Absent Achilles & Plantar |
| Tibial Nerve         | Popliteal Fossa/Posterior Leg | Paralysis of plantarflexion                     | Loss of sensation on sole/medial leg             | Absent Achilles   |
| Common Peroneal Nerve | Fibular Neck       | Paralysis of dorsiflexion                       | Loss of sensation on lateral leg/dorsum         | None              |
| Obturator Nerve      | Pelvis/Medial Thigh | Weakness in thigh adduction                      | Loss of sensation on medial thigh                | None              |
| Superior Gluteal Nerve| Gluteal Region     | Weakness in hip abduction                        | None                                            | None              |
| Femoral Nerve        | Inguinal Canal/Anterior Thigh | Weakness in knee extension/hip flexion          | Loss of sensation on anterior thigh/medial leg   | Patellar Reflex   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A lesion in the posterior thigh affecting both dorsiflexion and plantarflexion points to an injury of the sciatic nerve proximal to its bifurcation into the tibial and common peroneal nerves. Differentiating between sciatic nerve injury, tibial nerve injury, and common peroneal nerve injury relies on understanding the specific motor and sensory deficits associated with each nerve and the typical locations of injury. Remember the mnemonic "foot drop" is associated with common peroneal nerve injury (dorsiflexion weakness), while weakness in plantarflexion is associated with tibial nerve injury. Combined deficits indicate a more proximal lesion affecting the common trunk (sciatic nerve).

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 131
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential injuries associated with the sciatic nerve and its branches, specifically the common fibular (peroneal) nerve, within the popliteal fossa and its course around the fibular head, and differentiating it from other lower limb nerve injuries. (理解坐骨神经及其分支，特别是胫腓神经，在腘窝内及其绕胫骨头的走行，以及与其他下肢神经损伤的鉴别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to the posterior aspect of his left knee during a soccer game. He immediately felt a sharp pain radiating down the back of his leg. On examination, his left knee shows no signs of instability or ligamentous injury. Neurological assessment reveals intact sensation on the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot, but complete loss of sensation on the lateral aspect of the lower leg and dorsum of the foot. Motor examination shows an inability to dorsiflex the foot or evert the foot, with preserved plantarflexion and inversion strength. The patient is unable to walk on his heels. Ankle reflexes (Achilles tendon reflex) are intact bilaterally.

(A) Injury to the tibial nerve within the popliteal fossa.
(B) Injury to the common fibular (peroneal) nerve as it passes around the fibular head.
(C) Injury to the sural nerve distal to its formation.
(D) Injury to the obturator nerve high in the thigh.
(E) Injury to the femoral nerve proximal to the quadriceps femoris muscle.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Direct blow to the posterior knee (popliteal fossa). This location is critical as it houses the popliteal artery and vein, and the sciatic nerve divides into the tibial and common fibular nerves.
- **Symptoms:** Sharp pain radiating down the back of the leg suggests nerve involvement.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Loss of sensation on the *lateral* aspect of the lower leg and *dorsum* of the foot. This specific sensory distribution is characteristic of the common fibular nerve. The medial aspect of the leg and foot sensation is intact, ruling out significant tibial nerve involvement.
- **Motor Deficit:** Inability to dorsiflex (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus - innervated by deep fibular nerve, a branch of the common fibular nerve) and evert the foot (peroneus longus and brevis - innervated by the superficial fibular nerve, a branch of the common fibular nerve). Preserved plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus - tibial nerve) and inversion (tibialis posterior - tibial nerve) confirms the sparing of the tibial nerve.
- **Functional Deficit:** Inability to walk on heels (requires dorsiflexion) further points to a deficit in the muscles innervated by the common fibular nerve.
- **Reflexes:** Intact Achilles reflex (S1-S2, tibial nerve) indicates the tibial nerve root contribution and the nerve itself are likely intact proximal to the reflex arc.
- **Localization:** The combination of posterior knee trauma, lateral leg/dorsum foot sensory loss, and foot drop (inability to dorsiflex) strongly implicates injury to the common fibular nerve. The common fibular nerve is particularly vulnerable as it winds around the fibular head, a bony prominence.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve Division:** The sciatic nerve typically divides into the tibial and common fibular nerves in the popliteal fossa, posterior to the knee joint capsule, superior to the popliteal artery and vein.
- **Tibial Nerve:** Descends posteriorly, deep to the soleus muscle, supplying the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors, invertors) and providing sensation to the plantar surface of the foot. Injury would cause loss of plantarflexion, inversion, and plantar sensation, and loss of the Achilles reflex.
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** Wraps laterally around the fibular head, passing superficial to the extensor digitorum longus muscle. It then divides into the superficial and deep fibular nerves.
    - **Superficial Fibular Nerve:** Innervates the peroneus longus and brevis (eversion) and provides sensation to the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot (except the first web space).
    - **Deep Fibular Nerve:** Innervates the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, peroneus tertius - dorsiflexion and toe extension) and provides sensation to the first web space.
- **Vulnerability at Fibular Head:** The fibular head is a bony prominence, making the common fibular nerve susceptible to compression or direct trauma, especially with knee injuries or fractures. This is a classic location for common fibular nerve injury, leading to foot drop and sensory loss in its distribution.
- **Embryology:** The sciatic nerve and its branches arise from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3 roots). The common fibular nerve carries fibers primarily from L4, L5, and S1.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Injury to the tibial nerve within the popliteal fossa.** This would cause weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, sensory loss on the sole of the foot, and loss of the Achilles reflex. The patient has intact plantarflexion, inversion, and Achilles reflex, and sensory loss on the dorsum/lateral leg, making this incorrect.
- (C): **Injury to the sural nerve distal to its formation.** The sural nerve (formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves) provides sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot/heel. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, but *not* the characteristic foot drop or inability to dorsiflex/invert seen here. The sensory loss pattern described (lateral leg/dorsum foot) is distinct from the sural nerve distribution.
- (D): **Injury to the obturator nerve high in the thigh.** The obturator nerve innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh. Injury would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. This is inconsistent with the patient's presentation of foot drop and lateral leg sensory loss.
- (E): **Injury to the femoral nerve proximal to the quadriceps femoris muscle.** The femoral nerve innervates the quadriceps femoris (knee extension) and sartorius muscles, and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg (via the saphenous nerve). Injury would cause weakness in knee extension, inability to perform a straight leg raise, and sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg. This is inconsistent with the patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve Injury (Correct) | Tibial Nerve Injury (Distractor A) | Sural Nerve Injury (Distractor C) | Obturator Nerve Injury (Distractor D) | Femoral Nerve Injury (Distractor E) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Location of Injury** | Popliteal fossa/Fibular Head                      | Popliteal fossa                    | Distal leg                        | High thigh                            | Proximal thigh                      |
| **Motor Deficit**    | Foot drop (dorsiflexion), Eversion weakness        | Plantarflexion/Inversion weakness | None                              | Thigh adduction weakness              | Knee extension weakness              |
| **Sensory Deficit**  | Lateral leg, Dorsum foot (except 1st web space)   | Plantar foot                        | Posterolateral leg, Lateral foot    | Medial thigh                          | Anterior thigh, Medial leg           |
| **Reflexes**         | Normal (Achilles)                                 | Absent (Achilles)                   | Normal                            | Normal                                | Normal                               |
| **Key Muscles Affected**| Tibialis Anterior, EDL, Peroneus Longus/Brevis   | Gastrocnemius, Soleus, Tibialis Posterior | None                              | Adductors (Longus, Brevis, Magnus)    | Quadriceps Femoris, Sartorius       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular (peroneal) nerve is vulnerable to injury as it wraps around the fibular head, often due to direct trauma or compression. This results in foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexion and eversion) and sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot. Differentiating this from tibial nerve injury (plantarflexion/inversion weakness, plantar sensory loss) is crucial. Remember the mnemonic "DAVE" for common fibular nerve function: Dorsiflexion, Eversion, Sensory to the Anterolateral leg/Dorsum of the foot.

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 132
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the gluteal region, specifically the course of the sciatic nerve relative to the piriformis muscle, and the clinical implications of sciatic nerve injury or compression in this region. (理解臀区解剖关系，特别是坐骨神经相对于梨状肌的走行，以及坐骨神经在该区域损伤或受压的临床意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male marathon runner presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, radiating pain down his left posterior thigh and leg, beginning approximately 2 hours ago during his run. He reports a sudden onset of pain after feeling a sharp "pop" in his left buttock. On physical examination, he is unable to bear weight on the left leg. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in plantarflexion (MRC grade 2/5) and knee flexion (MRC grade 3/5) on the left side. Sensory examination shows decreased sensation over the lateral aspect of the left leg and the dorsum of the left foot. Patellar and Achilles reflexes are diminished on the left. An MRI of the lumbar spine and pelvis is ordered and reveals no evidence of lumbar disc herniation or spinal stenosis. However, the MRI shows edema and inflammation surrounding the piriformis muscle, with apparent compression of a major nerve bundle exiting the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen.

Which nerve is MOST likely compressed in this patient?

(A) Superior gluteal nerve
(B) Obturator nerve
(C) Pudendal nerve
(D) Sciatic nerve
(E) Femoral nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male marathon runner (suggests overuse injury or acute trauma during activity).
- **Chief Complaint:** Severe, radiating pain down the left posterior thigh and leg (consistent with sciatic nerve distribution).
- **Onset:** Sudden onset after a "pop" in the buttock (suggests acute injury, possibly muscle tear or nerve entrapment).
- **Physical Exam:**
    - Inability to bear weight (significant neurological deficit).
    - Weakness in plantarflexion (gastrocnemius/soleus, innervated by tibial division of sciatic nerve).
    - Weakness in knee flexion (hamstrings, innervated by tibial division of sciatic nerve).
    - Decreased sensation over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot (superficial peroneal nerve territory, innervated by common peroneal division of sciatic nerve).
    - Diminished patellar (L2-L4, femoral nerve) and Achilles (S1-S2, tibial nerve) reflexes (indicates involvement of L4-S2 nerve roots, which contribute to the sciatic nerve).
- **Imaging:** MRI rules out lumbar spine pathology but shows inflammation/edema around the piriformis muscle compressing a nerve bundle exiting the greater sciatic foramen.
- **Localization:** The symptoms (pain radiation, motor weakness, sensory loss, reflex changes) strongly point to a lesion affecting the sciatic nerve or its roots. The MRI findings localize the compression to the region of the piriformis muscle, where the sciatic nerve exits the pelvis.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve (坐骨神经):** The largest nerve in the body, formed by the union of the tibial (L4-S3) and common peroneal (L4-S2) divisions of the sacral plexus (L4-S3). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, typically inferior to the piriformis muscle.
- **Piriformis Muscle (梨状肌):** An external rotator of the hip, located deep in the gluteal region. The sciatic nerve usually passes *inferior* to the piriformis muscle. However, anatomical variations exist where the nerve may pass *through* the muscle, *superior* to the muscle, or *divided* by the muscle.
- **Piriformis Syndrome (梨状肌综合征):** A condition where the piriformis muscle spasms or tightens, compressing the sciatic nerve. This can cause pain, numbness, and tingling in the buttock and down the leg, mimicking sciatica from lumbar spine issues. The patient's presentation (acute onset after activity, buttock pain, sciatic distribution symptoms, MRI findings) is highly suggestive of piriformis syndrome causing sciatic nerve compression.
- **Superior Gluteal Nerve (上臀神经):** Innervates gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. Does not fit the patient's symptoms.
- **Obturator Nerve (闭孔神经):** Innervates medial thigh muscles (adductors). Injury causes weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. Does not fit the patient's symptoms.
- **Pudendal Nerve (会阴神经):** Innervates perineal muscles and provides sensation to the perineum and external genitalia. Injury causes saddle anesthesia and bowel/bladder dysfunction. Does not fit the patient's symptoms.
- **Femoral Nerve (股神经):** Innervates anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury causes weakness in knee extension and sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg. Does not fit the patient's symptoms.

##### 3. Correlation with Clinical Findings (临床发现关联)
- The patient's symptoms (pain radiating down posterior leg, weakness in plantarflexion and knee flexion, sensory loss on lateral leg/dorsum of foot, diminished Achilles reflex) are classic signs of sciatic nerve involvement.
- The MRI findings specifically point to compression of a nerve bundle near the piriformis muscle, consistent with the sciatic nerve's anatomical location.
- The "pop" sensation could represent a muscle strain (piriformis) or a sudden nerve compression event.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior gluteal nerve:** Innervates hip abductors (gluteus medius/minimus). Injury causes Trendelenburg gait (hip drop on contralateral side during stance phase). The patient's symptoms (posterior leg pain/weakness/sensory loss) are inconsistent with superior gluteal nerve injury.
- (B) **Obturator nerve:** Innervates medial thigh adductors. Injury causes weakness in adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. The patient's symptoms are in the posterior leg and foot, not the medial thigh.
- (C) **Pudendal nerve:** Innervates perineal structures. Injury causes saddle anesthesia and bowel/bladder dysfunction. The patient's symptoms are in the lower limb, not the perineum.
- (E) **Femoral nerve:** Innervates anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh/medial leg. Injury causes weakness in knee extension and sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg. The patient's symptoms are in the posterior leg and foot, not the anterior thigh.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Innervation/Function                                  | Sensory Distribution                                  | Motor Deficit                                       | Common Injury Mechanism/Syndrome          |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Sciatic Nerve (D)** | L4-S3; Tibial & Common Peroneal divisions             | Lateral leg, dorsum of foot, sole of foot             | Plantarflexion, knee flexion, foot eversion/dorsiflexion | Piriformis syndrome, hamstring injury, hip fracture |
| Superior Gluteal (A) | L4-S1                                                 | Over gluteus medius/minimus area                     | Hip abduction                                      | Hip dislocation, surgery                  |
| Obturator Nerve (B)  | L2-L4                                                 | Medial thigh                                          | Thigh adduction                                    | Pelvic fracture, hip surgery              |
| Pudendal Nerve (C)   | S2-S4                                                 | Perineum, external genitalia                          | Perineal muscles                                  | Childbirth, saddle injury, pelvic surgery |
| Femoral Nerve (E)    | L2-L4                                                 | Anterior thigh, medial leg                            | Knee extension, hip flexion                       | Hip fracture, retroperitoneal hematoma     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The sciatic nerve is the largest nerve in the body and is vulnerable to compression or injury in the gluteal region, particularly near the piriformis muscle (piriformis syndrome). Understanding the specific motor and sensory deficits associated with sciatic nerve injury is crucial for localization. Differentiating sciatic nerve pathology from lumbar radiculopathy or other peripheral nerve injuries requires careful clinical evaluation and localization of the lesion. Key differentials include superior gluteal nerve injury (hip abduction weakness), femoral nerve injury (knee extension weakness), and lumbar radiculopathy (often associated with back pain and specific dermatomal/myotomal patterns).
