<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 140
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential injury patterns in the posterior thigh, specifically involving the sciatic nerve and its branches (tibial and common peroneal/fibular nerves), and the muscles they innervate, particularly in the context of intramuscular injections. (理解股后肌群、坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经、腓神经）的解剖关系，以及肌内注射可能造成的损伤模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe pain and weakness in his left lower leg following a routine intramuscular injection of ceftriaxone administered by a nurse in the outpatient clinic. The injection was given into the "ventrogluteal" region, but the patient reports the nurse seemed unsure of the exact location. On examination, the patient exhibits significant weakness in plantarflexion of the foot and dorsiflexion of the ankle, along with sensory loss over the lateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot. Palpation reveals tenderness in the posterior aspect of the upper thigh. The patient is unable to walk on his toes or heels. An MRI of the left hip and thigh shows evidence of intramuscular hematoma and edema surrounding the sciatic nerve in the posterior thigh. Which structure is most likely injured by the injection?

(A) Femoral nerve
(B) Obturator nerve
(C) Sciatic nerve
(D) Tibial nerve
(E) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male with left lower leg pain and weakness after an intramuscular injection. This points towards a potential nerve injury related to the injection site.
- **Injection Site:** "Ventrogluteal" region, but the nurse was unsure. The ventrogluteal site is generally considered safe, but improper technique or anatomical variation can lead to injury. The posterior thigh tenderness suggests the injury occurred deeper than the subcutaneous tissue.
- **Motor Deficits:** Weakness in plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus - innervated by tibial nerve) and dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus - innervated by deep peroneal nerve, a branch of the common peroneal nerve). This indicates involvement of both the tibial and common peroneal nerves.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Sensory loss over the lateral lower leg (superficial peroneal nerve) and dorsum of the foot (deep peroneal nerve). This further supports common peroneal nerve involvement.
- **Functional Deficit:** Inability to walk on toes (plantarflexion, tibial nerve) or heels (dorsiflexion, common peroneal nerve).
- **Imaging:** MRI shows intramuscular hematoma and edema around the sciatic nerve in the posterior thigh. This directly localizes the injury.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of motor and sensory deficits affecting both the tibial and common peroneal nerve distributions, along with the MRI findings, strongly implicates injury to the common parent nerve, the sciatic nerve, in the posterior thigh.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve (坐骨神经):** The largest nerve in the body, formed in the pelvis by the union of the tibial (L4-S3) and common peroneal (L4-S2) divisions of the sacral plexus. It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle.
- **Course in Thigh:** It descends deep to the gluteus maximus muscle in the posterior thigh, running between the biceps femoris laterally and the semimembranosus/semitendinosus medially. It typically divides into the tibial and common peroneal nerves at the upper border of the adductor magnus muscle, near the popliteal fossa.
- **Branches:** Before dividing, the sciatic nerve gives off branches to the posterior thigh muscles (hamstrings: biceps femoris long head, semitendinosus, semimembranosus) and the superior and inferior gluteal nerves (which innervate gluteus medius, minimus, and maximus).
- **Vulnerability:** The sciatic nerve is vulnerable to injury in the posterior thigh, particularly during intramuscular injections, hip surgery, or trauma. The ventrogluteal site is chosen specifically to avoid the sciatic nerve, but improper technique or anatomical variations can lead to injury. Intramuscular injections in the posterior thigh (e.g., into the gluteus medius or maximus) carry a higher risk of sciatic nerve injury.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the sciatic nerve in the posterior thigh can cause weakness and sensory loss in the distribution of both the tibial and common peroneal nerves, as seen in this patient. The MRI findings confirm the location of the injury.
- **Embryology:** The sacral plexus (L4-S3) gives rise to the sciatic nerve. The tibial and common peroneal divisions arise from the anterior and posterior divisions of the sacral plexus, respectively.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Femoral nerve (股神经):** Arises from the lumbar plexus (L2-L4). It passes under the inguinal ligament into the anterior thigh. It innervates the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury would cause quadriceps weakness (inability to extend the knee) and sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg. This does not match the patient's posterior thigh/lower leg deficits.
- (B) **Obturator nerve (闭孔神经):** Arises from the lumbar plexus (L2-L4). It passes through the obturator foramen to enter the medial thigh. It innervates the medial thigh muscles (adductors) and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Injury would cause adductor weakness and sensory loss in the medial thigh. This does not match the patient's posterior thigh/lower leg deficits.
- (D) **Tibial nerve (胫神经):** A terminal branch of the sciatic nerve. It runs down the posterior leg, innervating the posterior compartment muscles (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus) and providing sensation to the sole of the foot. Injury *after* the sciatic nerve bifurcation would cause weakness in plantarflexion and sensory loss on the sole, but would spare dorsiflexion and lateral leg sensation. The patient has deficits in *both* tibial and common peroneal distributions, indicating an injury *proximal* to the bifurcation.
- (E) **Common peroneal (fibular) nerve (腓神经):** A terminal branch of the sciatic nerve. It wraps around the fibular neck and divides into superficial and deep peroneal nerves. It innervates the anterior and lateral compartment muscles of the leg (tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus, peroneus longus, peroneus brevis) and provides sensation to the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg. Injury *after* the sciatic nerve bifurcation would cause weakness in dorsiflexion and eversion, and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg, but would spare plantarflexion. The patient has deficits in *both* tibial and common peroneal distributions, indicating an injury *proximal* to the bifurcation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Sciatic Nerve (坐骨神经) | Femoral Nerve (股神经) | Obturator Nerve (闭孔神经) | Tibial Nerve (胫神经) | Common Peroneal Nerve (腓神经) |
|----------------------|--------------------------|------------------------|--------------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------------|
| **Plexus Origin**    | Sacral (L4-S3)           | Lumbar (L2-L4)         | Lumbar (L2-L4)           | Sacral (L4-S3)       | Sacral (L4-S2)                  |
| **Location Injured** | Posterior Thigh          | Anterior Thigh         | Medial Thigh             | Posterior Leg         | Lateral Leg (around fibular neck) |
| **Motor Deficits**   | Plantarflexion, Dorsiflexion, Eversion, Inversion | Knee Extension, Hip Flexion | Hip Adduction         | Plantarflexion, Inversion      | Dorsiflexion, Eversion          |
| **Sensory Deficits** | Lateral Leg, Dorsum Foot, Sole Foot | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg | Medial Thigh            | Sole Foot             | Dorsum Foot, Lateral Leg        |
| **Key Muscles Inn.** | Hamstrings, Gluteals     | Quadriceps, Sartorius  | Adductors                | Gastrocnemius, Soleus | Tibialis Anterior, Peronei      |
| **Injury Cause**     | Intramuscular Injection (Posterior Thigh), Hip Fracture | Hernia Repair, Hip Fracture | Hip Fracture, Pelvic Surgery | Trauma (Popliteal Fossa)        | Fibular Neck Fracture, Compression |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The sciatic nerve is the largest nerve in the body and is vulnerable to injury in the posterior thigh, especially during intramuscular injections. Injury proximal to its bifurcation into the tibial and common peroneal nerves results in deficits in both nerve distributions. Differentiating sciatic nerve injury from injuries to its terminal branches or other major lower limb nerves (femoral, obturator) relies on understanding their anatomical courses, innervation patterns, and common injury mechanisms. Remember the "ventrogluteal" site is designed to avoid the sciatic nerve, but improper technique can still cause injury.

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 141
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the posterior thigh, specifically the sciatic nerve and its divisions (tibial and common peroneal/fibular nerves), and the muscles they innervate, as well as the potential for injury during intramuscular injections. (理解股后部解剖关系，特别是坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和普通腓神经/尺腓神经），以及肌内注射时潜在的损伤风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe pain and weakness in his right lower leg following a routine intramuscular injection of ceftriaxone administered into the right gluteus medius muscle by a nurse at an outpatient clinic. The patient reports immediate, sharp pain radiating down the posterior aspect of his thigh, followed by numbness and tingling in his foot. On physical examination, his right leg demonstrates significant weakness in plantarflexion (foot pointing down) and inversion (turning the sole of the foot inward). Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the lateral aspect of the foot and the sole. The ankle jerk reflex (Achilles reflex) is absent on the right side. The nurse reports difficulty locating the gluteus medius muscle due to the patient's obesity and administered the injection slightly lower than the intended site.

(A) Superior gluteal nerve
(B) Femoral nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Tibial nerve
(E) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Intramuscular injection into gluteus medius:** This points to the gluteal region as the site of injury. The injection was administered slightly lower than intended, suggesting potential involvement of structures deep to the gluteus medius or adjacent muscles.
- **Immediate, sharp pain radiating down the posterior thigh:** This suggests irritation or injury to a major nerve trunk passing through the posterior thigh.
- **Numbness and tingling in the foot:** Indicates sensory nerve involvement.
- **Weakness in plantarflexion (foot pointing down) and inversion (turning the sole of the foot inward):** Plantarflexion is primarily mediated by the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles, innervated by the tibial nerve. Inversion is primarily mediated by the tibialis posterior muscle, also innervated by the tibial nerve. This points strongly to tibial nerve dysfunction.
- **Decreased sensation over the lateral aspect of the foot and the sole:** The tibial nerve provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot. The lateral aspect of the foot is innervated by the sural nerve (a branch of the tibial nerve and common peroneal nerve), and the lateral border is innervated by the common peroneal nerve. However, the *sole* sensation loss is a key indicator of tibial nerve involvement.
- **Absent ankle jerk reflex (Achilles reflex):** This reflex tests the integrity of the S1 nerve root, the tibial nerve, and the gastrocnemius/soleus muscles. An absent reflex confirms a lesion affecting this pathway, most likely the tibial nerve itself.
- **Location of injection (gluteus medius, slightly low):** The sciatic nerve passes deep to the gluteus maximus and through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle. It then travels down the posterior thigh, typically deep to the long head of the biceps femoris. An injection into the gluteus medius, especially if slightly low, can potentially injure the sciatic nerve or its branches as they pass through this region. The sciatic nerve divides into the tibial and common peroneal nerves usually in the posterior thigh, often near the popliteal fossa. Injury *before* this division would affect both branches, but the symptoms described (plantarflexion/inversion weakness, sole sensation loss, absent ankle reflex) are classic for tibial nerve damage.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve:** The largest nerve in the body, originating from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle. It runs down the posterior thigh, deep to the gluteus maximus and biceps femoris (long head).
- **Sciatic Nerve Division:** Typically divides into the tibial nerve and the common peroneal (fibular) nerve in the posterior thigh, often near the popliteal fossa, although the exact location can vary.
- **Tibial Nerve:** The larger branch, it continues down the posterior leg, deep to the soleus muscle. It innervates the posterior compartment muscles of the leg (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus) responsible for plantarflexion and inversion. It provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot and the posterior aspect of the lower leg. It carries the reflex arc for the Achilles tendon reflex (S1).
- **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve:** Wraps around the fibular head, becoming superficial. It innervates the anterior and lateral compartment muscles of the leg (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, peroneus tertius, peroneus longus, peroneus brevis) responsible for dorsiflexion and eversion. It provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum and lateral aspect of the foot.
- **Gluteal Region Anatomy:** The gluteus medius muscle lies superior and anterior to the gluteus maximus. The sciatic nerve passes deep to the gluteus maximus. An injection into the gluteus medius, especially if too low or lateral, can potentially injure the sciatic nerve or its branches as they traverse the region. The superior gluteal nerve exits the pelvis superior to the piriformis muscle and innervates the gluteus medius and minimus; injury would cause hip abduction weakness, not the symptoms described.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, sensory loss on the sole, absent ankle reflex) are pathognomonic for tibial nerve injury. The mechanism (intramuscular injection in the gluteal region) is a known cause of sciatic nerve or tibial nerve damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior gluteal nerve:** Innervates gluteus medius and minimus. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. This does not match the patient's lower leg symptoms. The injection site (gluteus medius) is innervated by this nerve, but injury to the nerve itself wouldn't cause the described foot/leg deficits.
- (B) **Femoral nerve:** Arises from L2-L4. Innervates the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury causes quadriceps weakness (difficulty extending the knee) and sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg. This does not match the patient's posterior leg/foot symptoms.
- (C) **Obturator nerve:** Arises from L2-L4. Passes through the obturator foramen to innervate the medial thigh muscles (adductors) and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Injury causes adductor weakness and sensory loss in the medial thigh. This does not match the patient's posterior leg/foot symptoms.
- (E) **Common peroneal (fibular) nerve:** A branch of the sciatic nerve. Injury typically occurs as it wraps around the fibular head. Causes weakness in dorsiflexion and eversion (foot drop) and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot and anterolateral leg. While it's a branch of the sciatic nerve and could potentially be injured alongside the tibial nerve, the specific symptoms described (plantarflexion/inversion weakness, sole sensation loss, absent ankle reflex) point definitively to the tibial nerve. Injury to the common peroneal nerve alone would not cause these findings.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Tibial Nerve (Correct)                                  | Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve (Distractor E)              | Femoral Nerve (Distractor B)                               | Obturator Nerve (Distractor C)                             | Superior Gluteal Nerve (Distractor A)                       |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Origin**              | Lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3), branch of Sciatic Nerve     | Lumbosacral plexus (L4-S2), branch of Sciatic Nerve        | Lumbosacral plexus (L2-L4)                                 | Lumbosacral plexus (L2-L4)                                 | Lumbosacral plexus (L4-S1)                                  |
| **Course**              | Posterior thigh -> Posterior leg -> Foot                | Posterior thigh -> Wraps fibular head -> Anterior/Lateral leg | Anterior thigh                                             | Medial thigh (via obturator foramen)                        | Gluteal region -> Gluteus medius/minimus                    |
| **Motor Innervation**   | Posterior leg muscles (plantarflexors, invertors)       | Anterior/Lateral leg muscles (dorsiflexors, everters)      | Anterior thigh muscles (knee extensors, hip flexors)       | Medial thigh muscles (adductors)                           | Gluteus medius, minimus (hip abductors)                     |
| **Sensory Innervation** | Sole of foot, posterior leg                             | Dorsum of foot, anterolateral leg                          | Anterior thigh, medial leg                                 | Medial thigh                                               | Lateral hip, upper lateral thigh                             |
| **Key Reflex**          | Achilles (ankle jerk) - S1                               | None specific                                              | Patellar (knee jerk) - L3/L4                               | None specific                                              | None specific                                               |
| **Clinical Deficit**    | Weak plantarflexion/inversion, sole sensory loss, ↓Achilles | Weak dorsiflexion/eversion, foot drop, dorsum sensory loss | Weak knee extension, quadriceps atrophy, anterior sensory loss | Weak adduction, medial sensory loss                         | Weak hip abduction, Trendelenburg gait, lateral sensory loss |
| **Injury Mechanism**    | Gluteal injection (low), Popliteal fossa injury          | Fibular head fracture/trauma, compression                  | Hip surgery, retroperitoneal hematoma                       | Hip surgery, obturator hernia                               | Hip surgery, gluteal injection (high/lateral)               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Intramuscular injections in the gluteal region carry a risk of sciatic nerve injury, particularly if administered improperly. The sciatic nerve divides into the tibial and common peroneal nerves, typically in the posterior thigh. Injury to the tibial nerve results in weakness of plantarflexion and inversion, sensory loss on the sole of the foot, and loss of the Achilles reflex. Differentiating tibial nerve injury from common peroneal nerve injury (foot drop, dorsum sensory loss) and other lower limb nerve injuries (femoral, obturator) is crucial based on the specific motor and sensory deficits.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 142
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential injuries associated with the sciatic nerve and its branches, specifically the common fibular (peroneal) nerve, and its vulnerability in the context of lower limb trauma or positioning. (理解坐骨神经及其分支，特别是胫腓神经的解剖关系及其在下肢外伤或体位中的潜在损伤风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed fracture of the mid-shaft of his left fibula during a fall. He reports immediate onset of numbness and tingling along the lateral aspect of his lower leg and dorsum of his foot. On physical examination, he demonstrates significant weakness in dorsiflexion of the foot and eversion of the foot, with a foot drop noted. Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the lateral lower leg and the dorsum of the foot, sparing the medial aspect of the foot and the plantar surface. Ankle reflexes (Achilles and plantar) are intact. Radiographs confirm a displaced fracture of the mid-shaft fibula. Which nerve is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Obturator nerve
(E) Saphenous nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with a closed mid-shaft fibula fracture. This immediately points towards potential injury to structures closely associated with the fibula.
- **Symptoms:** Numbness and tingling along the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This sensory distribution is characteristic of the common fibular (peroneal) nerve.
- **Physical Examination:** Weakness in dorsiflexion (foot drop) and eversion. Dorsiflexion is primarily innervated by the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve (branch of common fibular), and eversion is innervated by the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve (branch of common fibular). Foot drop is a classic sign of common fibular nerve injury.
- **Sensory Examination:** Decreased sensation over the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot, sparing the medial foot and plantar surface. This sensory distribution matches the common fibular nerve territory.
- **Reflexes:** Intact ankle (Achilles) and plantar reflexes. The Achilles reflex tests the tibial nerve (S1), and the plantar reflex tests the tibial nerve (S1-S3). Intact reflexes suggest the tibial nerve is likely unaffected.
- **Fracture Location:** Mid-shaft fibula fracture. The common fibular nerve wraps around the neck of the fibula before passing anteriorly into the anterior compartment of the leg. This location makes it highly vulnerable to injury with fibular fractures, especially near the fibular head/neck.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** A branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S2). It arises in the posterior thigh, passes through the popliteal fossa, and winds around the lateral aspect of the fibular neck, just posterior to the fibular head. It then enters the anterior compartment of the leg, passing between the fibularis longus and brevis muscles.
- **Branches:** It divides into the superficial fibular nerve (innervates fibularis longus and brevis muscles for eversion; sensory to lateral leg and dorsum of foot) and the deep fibular nerve (innervates anterior compartment muscles for dorsiflexion, toe extension, and foot inversion; sensory to the web space between the first and second toes).
- **Vulnerability:** Its superficial course around the fibular neck makes it susceptible to injury from direct trauma, fractures of the fibular neck or head, compression (e.g., prolonged leg crossing), or entrapment.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Injury typically causes foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexion), sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot, and weakness of eversion. The specific deficits depend on whether the injury occurs proximal to the bifurcation (affecting both superficial and deep branches) or distal to it (affecting only one branch). In this case, the patient has both dorsiflexion and eversion weakness, along with sensory loss in the typical distribution, suggesting injury to the common fibular nerve proximal to or at its bifurcation.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The tibial nerve runs posterior to the medial head of the gastrocnemius muscle and deep to the soleus muscle in the posterior compartment. Injury would cause weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, and sensory loss on the plantar surface and medial foot. The sural nerve provides sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and lateral foot. The obturator nerve innervates the medial thigh compartment. The saphenous nerve innervates the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot. None of these match the patient's presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** This nerve runs posteriorly in the leg, deep to the soleus muscle. It innervates the plantar flexors and provides sensation to the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to plantarflex or invert the foot, and sensory loss on the plantar surface. This is inconsistent with the patient's foot drop, eversion weakness, and sensory loss pattern. The tibial nerve is less likely to be injured by a mid-shaft fibula fracture.
- (B) **Sural nerve:** This nerve is formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs superficially down the posterolateral aspect of the leg and provides sensation to the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution but would not cause motor weakness like foot drop or eversion weakness.
- (D) **Obturator nerve:** This nerve innervates the adductor muscles of the medial thigh. Injury would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. It is located in the pelvis and thigh, far from the site of injury and the observed deficits.
- (E) **Saphenous nerve:** This is a branch of the femoral nerve. It runs down the medial aspect of the leg, accompanying the great saphenous vein. It provides sensation to the medial lower leg and foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution but no significant motor weakness related to the foot/ankle.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve | Tibial Nerve                  | Sural Nerve                   | Obturator Nerve              | Saphenous Nerve              |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------|-------------------------------|-------------------------------|-------------------------------|
| **Origin**           | Sciatic Nerve (L4-S2)           | Sciatic Nerve (L4-S2)         | Tibial & Common Fibular Nerves| Lumbar Plexus (L2-L4)        | Femoral Nerve (L2-L4)        |
| **Course**           | Wraps fibular neck, anterior leg| Posterior leg, deep to soleus | Posterolateral leg            | Medial thigh                  | Medial leg                    |
| **Motor Innervation**| Dorsiflexors, Evertors         | Plantarflexors, Invertors     | None                          | Adductors                     | Tibialis Anterior (partial)   |
| **Sensory Innervation**| Lateral leg, Dorsum of foot    | Plantar surface, Medial foot  | Posterolateral leg, Lateral foot| Medial thigh                  | Medial leg, Medial foot       |
| **Key Deficit**      | Foot Drop, Eversion Weakness    | Plantarflexion Weakness       | Sensory Loss (lateral foot)   | Thigh Adduction Weakness     | Sensory Loss (medial leg/foot)|
| **Vulnerability**    | Fibular neck/head fracture     | Popliteal fossa injury        | Superficial course            | Pelvic/Thigh trauma           | Medial leg trauma             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular (peroneal) nerve is highly susceptible to injury where it wraps around the fibular neck, particularly with fibular fractures. This injury classically presents with foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexion due to deep fibular nerve involvement) and sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot (superficial fibular nerve involvement). Differentiating it from tibial nerve injury (plantarflexion weakness, plantar sensory loss) is crucial. Remember the mnemonic "DEEP" for dorsiflexion and eversion (deep fibular nerve) and "SUPERFICIAL" for sensation over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot (superficial fibular nerve).

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 143
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the posterior thigh, specifically the sciatic nerve and its divisions (tibial and common peroneal/fibular nerves), and the muscles they innervate, to localize a lesion based on motor deficits. (理解股后侧的解剖关系，特别是坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和普通腓神经/尺腓神经），以及它们支配的肌肉，以根据运动功能障碍定位病变。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department after accidentally falling from scaffolding and landing directly on his right buttock. He complains of severe right lower back and buttock pain radiating down his right leg. On examination, his right lower limb exhibits significant weakness in plantar flexion (foot pointing downwards) and inversion (turning the sole of the foot inwards). Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation on the lateral aspect of the right foot and the sole. He is unable to stand on his toes on the affected side (positive Thompson test). Ankle reflexes (Achilles tendon reflex) are absent on the right. Knee flexion and hamstring function are intact. Hip flexion and extension are also normal. An MRI of the lumbar spine and pelvis shows no evidence of spinal cord compression or nerve root impingement. However, an MRI of the right thigh reveals a hematoma compressing the sciatic nerve in the mid-thigh region. Which nerve division is most likely compressed by the hematoma, leading to this specific clinical presentation?

(A) Tibial nerve proximal to its division from the sciatic nerve.
(B) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve proximal to its division from the sciatic nerve.
(C) Tibial nerve distal to its division from the sciatic nerve.
(D) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve distal to its division from the sciatic nerve.
(E) Superior gluteal nerve.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Fall onto buttock, suggesting trauma to the posterior hip/thigh region.
- **Pain Pattern:** Right buttock pain radiating down the right leg, consistent with sciatic nerve irritation or compression.
- **Motor Deficits:**
    - Weakness in plantar flexion: This action is primarily performed by the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles.
    - Weakness in inversion: This action is primarily performed by the tibialis posterior muscle.
    - Intact knee flexion and hamstring function: Hamstrings (biceps femoris, semitendinosus, semimembranosus) are innervated by the tibial nerve (except the short head of biceps femoris, innervated by the common peroneal nerve). Intact function suggests the tibial nerve is not completely transected *before* it innervates the hamstrings.
    - Intact hip flexion/extension: These are innervated by nerves originating from the lumbar plexus (femoral, obturator, superior gluteal nerves), ruling out involvement of these nerves.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Decreased sensation on the lateral aspect of the foot and the sole. The lateral foot is innervated by the sural nerve (derived from tibial and common peroneal nerves), and the sole is innervated by the medial and lateral plantar nerves (branches of the tibial nerve). The combination points strongly towards tibial nerve involvement.
- **Reflexes:** Absent Achilles tendon reflex (ankle jerk), which tests the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles (innervated by the tibial nerve).
- **Imaging:** MRI shows a hematoma compressing the sciatic nerve in the mid-thigh. This localizes the lesion to the thigh, distal to the piriformis muscle where the sciatic nerve typically exits the pelvis. MRI rules out spinal cord/root issues.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of impaired plantar flexion, inversion, sensation on the sole and lateral foot, and absent Achilles reflex strongly implicates the tibial nerve. The lesion is in the mid-thigh, distal to the sciatic nerve bifurcation. Therefore, the compression must be affecting the tibial nerve *after* it has separated from the common peroneal nerve.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sciatic Nerve:** The largest nerve in the body, originating from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, typically inferior to the piriformis muscle.
- **Bifurcation:** The sciatic nerve usually divides into the tibial nerve and the common peroneal (fibular) nerve in the posterior thigh, often around the popliteal fossa or slightly proximal to it.
- **Tibial Nerve:** Runs deep in the posterior compartment of the leg. It innervates the posterior compartment muscles (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus) and provides sensation to the posterior leg, the sole of the foot, and part of the lateral foot (via the sural nerve). It is responsible for plantar flexion and inversion of the foot, and flexion of the toes. The Achilles reflex tests the S1 nerve root via the tibial nerve's innervation of the gastrocnemius and soleus.
- **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve:** Wraps around the fibular head and divides into the superficial peroneal nerve (innervates lateral compartment muscles - peroneus longus and brevis, responsible for eversion) and the deep peroneal nerve (innervates anterior compartment muscles - tibialis anterior, extensor digitorum longus, extensor hallucis longus, peroneus tertius, responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension). It provides sensation to the anterolateral leg and the dorsum of the foot.
- **Lesion Localization:** The patient's symptoms (weak plantar flexion, inversion, sole/lateral foot sensation loss, absent Achilles reflex) are classic signs of tibial nerve dysfunction. The MRI confirms a lesion in the mid-thigh affecting the sciatic nerve *after* its bifurcation. Therefore, the tibial nerve division must be the affected structure.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Tibial nerve proximal to its division from the sciatic nerve: If the tibial nerve were compressed *before* the bifurcation, the common peroneal nerve would also be affected. This would lead to weakness in dorsiflexion and eversion (foot drop), in addition to the observed plantar flexion and inversion weakness. The sensory loss pattern would also be different, potentially including the anterolateral leg. This doesn't fit the clinical picture.
- (B) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve proximal to its division from the sciatic nerve: Compression here would affect both the tibial and common peroneal nerves. This would cause weakness in plantar flexion, inversion, dorsiflexion, and eversion (foot drop), along with widespread sensory loss in the leg and foot. This is inconsistent with the patient's presentation, which spares dorsiflexion and eversion.
- (D) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve distal to its division from the sciatic nerve: Compression of the common peroneal nerve (or its branches) would cause weakness in dorsiflexion and eversion (foot drop) and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot and anterolateral leg. This is the opposite of the patient's symptoms.
- (E) Superior gluteal nerve: This nerve innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Damage would cause weakness in hip abduction and internal rotation, and Trendelenburg gait. It does not affect the lower leg or foot function, making it inconsistent with the patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Tibial Nerve (Distal to Bifurcation) | Common Peroneal Nerve (Distal to Bifurcation) | Sciatic Nerve (Proximal to Bifurcation) | Superior Gluteal Nerve |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|------------------------|
| **Location**         | Posterior thigh/leg                  | Lateral thigh/leg (wraps fibular head)       | Posterior thigh/pelvis                  | Gluteal region         |
| **Motor Function**   | Plantar flexion, inversion, toe flexion | Dorsiflexion, eversion, toe extension        | Plantar flexion, inversion, dorsiflexion, eversion, toe flexion/extension | Hip abduction, internal rotation |
| **Sensory Function** | Sole, posterior leg, lateral foot      | Dorsum foot, anterolateral leg              | Sole, posterior leg, lateral foot, dorsum foot, anterolateral leg | Gluteal region         |
| **Reflexes**         | Achilles (S1)                         | None specific                                | None specific                           | None specific          |
| **Patient Symptoms** | Weak plantar flexion/inversion, sole/lateral foot sensory loss, absent Achilles reflex | Weak dorsiflexion/eversion, dorsum foot sensory loss | Weakness/sensory loss in entire leg/foot | Hip abduction weakness |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of weakness in plantar flexion and inversion, sensory loss on the sole and lateral foot, and absent Achilles reflex points specifically to tibial nerve dysfunction. Differentiating between lesions affecting the sciatic nerve proximal to its bifurcation versus its divisions (tibial and common peroneal) requires careful analysis of the specific motor and sensory deficits. Remember that the tibial nerve innervates the posterior compartment muscles and provides sensation to the sole, while the common peroneal nerve innervates the anterior and lateral compartments and provides sensation to the dorsum. Lesions proximal to the bifurcation affect both nerves.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 144
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the gluteal region, specifically the course of the sciatic nerve relative to the piriformis muscle, and the clinical implications of sciatic nerve injury or compression. (理解臀区解剖关系，特别是坐骨神经相对于梨状肌的走行，以及坐骨神经损伤或压迫的临床意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to his right buttock while operating a jackhammer. He reports immediate, severe pain radiating down the posterior aspect of his right leg, accompanied by numbness and tingling in his foot. On physical examination, his right lower limb exhibits significant weakness in plantar flexion (MRC grade 2/5), dorsiflexion (MRC grade 3/5), and eversion (MRC grade 3/5). Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the lateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot. The Achilles reflex (S1) and plantar reflex (S1) are absent on the right side. An MRI of the lumbar spine and pelvis reveals no evidence of disc herniation or spinal stenosis. However, an MRI of the right hip shows edema and inflammation surrounding the piriformis muscle, with apparent compression of a major nerve bundle exiting the pelvis.

Which nerve is most likely compressed by the inflamed piriformis muscle, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Pudendal nerve
(B) Femoral nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Sciatic nerve
(E) Superior gluteal nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Direct blow to the buttock. This localizes the potential injury to the gluteal region.
- **Symptoms:** Severe pain radiating down the posterior leg, numbness/tingling in the foot. This suggests involvement of a major nerve supplying the posterior lower limb.
- **Motor Deficits:** Weakness in plantar flexion (gastrocnemius, soleus - tibial nerve component), dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus - common peroneal/fibular nerve component), and eversion (peroneus longus, peroneus brevis - common peroneal/fibular nerve component). This pattern indicates involvement of multiple nerve roots/branches supplying the posterior and lateral compartments of the lower leg and foot.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Decreased sensation over the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This corresponds to the distribution of the common peroneal/fibular nerve.
- **Reflexes:** Absent Achilles reflex (S1) and plantar reflex (S1). This points to involvement of the S1 nerve root or its terminal branches (tibial nerve).
- **Imaging:** MRI shows inflammation around the piriformis muscle compressing a nerve bundle exiting the pelvis. This directly implicates the piriformis muscle and its relationship with nearby nerves.
- **Exclusion:** MRI of the lumbar spine is normal, ruling out radiculopathy from disc herniation or spinal stenosis as the primary cause.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Piriformis Muscle:** Located deep in the gluteal region, it originates from the anterior surface of the sacrum and inserts onto the greater trochanter of the femur. It acts as an external rotator of the hip.
- **Sciatic Nerve:** The largest nerve in the body, formed by the union of the tibial nerve (L4-S3) and common peroneal/fibular nerve (L4-S2) in the upper part of the posterior thigh, typically around the level of the piriformis muscle.
- **Relationship:** The sciatic nerve typically exits the pelvis *inferior* to the piriformis muscle. However, anatomical variations exist, with the sciatic nerve passing *through* the piriformis muscle (most common variation, ~70%), *superior* to it, or *between* its heads.
- **Piriformis Syndrome:** A condition where the piriformis muscle spasms or becomes tight, compressing the sciatic nerve as it passes nearby or through the muscle. This causes pain radiating down the posterior leg (sciatica), often exacerbated by sitting or activities involving hip rotation.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (pain radiating down posterior leg, weakness in plantar flexion, dorsiflexion, eversion, sensory loss in lateral leg/dorsum of foot, absent Achilles reflex) are classic signs of sciatic nerve dysfunction affecting its tibial and common peroneal components. The MRI findings of piriformis inflammation compressing a nerve bundle strongly support piriformis syndrome causing sciatic nerve compression.
- **Embryology:** The sciatic nerve derives from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3). The piriformis muscle develops from the posterior part of the first somite.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Pudendal nerve:** This nerve (S2-S4) supplies the perineum and external genitalia. Injury would cause saddle anesthesia, loss of anal sphincter tone, and erectile dysfunction. The patient's symptoms are in the lower limb, not the perineum. The pudendal nerve exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen inferior to the piriformis muscle, but its distribution does not match the clinical picture.
- (B) **Femoral nerve:** This nerve (L2-L4) supplies the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury would cause weakness in knee extension and hip flexion, and sensory loss in the anterior thigh and medial leg. The patient's deficits are in the posterior and lateral lower leg/foot. The femoral nerve passes under the inguinal ligament, far from the piriformis muscle.
- (C) **Obturator nerve:** This nerve (L2-L4) supplies the medial thigh muscles (adductors) and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Injury would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss in the medial thigh. The patient's deficits are in the posterior and lateral lower leg/foot. The obturator nerve exits the pelvis through the obturator foramen, also distant from the piriformis muscle.
- (E) **Superior gluteal nerve:** This nerve (L4-S1) supplies the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae muscles. Injury would cause weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. While located in the gluteal region, its distribution does not explain the patient's lower leg and foot symptoms. It exits the pelvis superior to the piriformis muscle.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure           | Origin/Innervation | Function                                  | Clinical Deficit (Injury)                                                                 | Relationship to Piriformis | Relevance to Vignette |
|--------------------|--------------------|-------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Sciatic Nerve**  | L4-S3              | Motor/Sensory to posterior/lateral leg/foot | Weakness in plantar flexion, dorsiflexion, eversion; sensory loss lateral leg/dorsum foot; absent Achilles reflex | Passes near/through       | **Correct**           |
| Pudendal Nerve     | S2-S4              | Motor/Sensory to perineum                 | Saddle anesthesia, loss of anal tone, erectile dysfunction                               | Exits inferiorly           | Incorrect             |
| Femoral Nerve      | L2-L4              | Motor/Sensory to anterior thigh/medial leg | Weakness in knee extension, hip flexion; sensory loss anterior thigh/medial leg           | Distal (inguinal ligament) | Incorrect             |
| Obturator Nerve    | L2-L4              | Motor/Sensory to medial thigh              | Weakness in thigh adduction; sensory loss medial thigh                                   | Exits via obturator foramen | Incorrect             |
| Superior Gluteal N | L4-S1              | Motor to gluteus medius/minimus/TFL       | Weakness in hip abduction (Trendelenburg gait)                                            | Exits superiorly           | Incorrect             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of pain radiating down the posterior leg with weakness in plantar flexion, dorsiflexion, eversion, sensory loss in the lateral leg/dorsum of the foot, and absent Achilles reflex strongly suggests sciatic nerve involvement. The MRI findings localizing inflammation around the piriformis muscle compressing the nerve points to piriformis syndrome as the likely cause. Key differentials for lower limb neurological deficits include lumbar radiculopathy (ruled out by normal lumbar MRI), peripheral neuropathy, and other entrapment neuropathies (e.g., peroneal nerve entrapment at the fibular head). Understanding the anatomical course of the sciatic nerve relative to the piriformis muscle is crucial for diagnosing piriformis syndrome.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 145
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential vulnerabilities of the sciatic nerve and its branches (tibial and common peroneal/fibular nerves) within the popliteal fossa and distal leg, specifically relating to compartment syndromes and nerve injury patterns. (理解坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和腓神经）在腘窝和下腿远端的解剖关系及其潜在脆弱性，特别是与深层筋膜室综合征和神经损伤模式的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male marathon runner presents to the emergency department after collapsing during a race. He reports severe pain and rapidly increasing swelling in his left lower leg, starting shortly after the collapse. On examination, his left leg is diffusely swollen, tense, and exquisitely tender to palpation, particularly in the posterior compartment. Distal pulses (dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial) are palpable but weak. Neurological examination reveals intact sensation on the medial aspect of the foot but absent sensation on the lateral aspect of the foot and dorsum of the foot. Motor examination shows weakness in plantarflexion (foot pointing down) and inversion (turning sole inward) of the foot, but normal dorsiflexion (foot pointing up) and eversion (turning sole outward). Ankle reflexes (Achilles tendon reflex) are absent on the left. An emergent fasciotomy is performed on the posterior compartment of the left leg. During the procedure, the surgeon notes significant muscle swelling and discoloration. Which nerve is most likely compromised by the compartment syndrome in this patient?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve
(C) Sural nerve
(D) Saphenous nerve
(E) Superficial peroneal (fibular) nerve

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male marathon runner - suggests overuse injury or vascular/neurological compromise related to exertion.
- **Chief Complaint:** Severe pain and rapidly increasing swelling in the left lower leg after collapse - classic signs of acute compartment syndrome.
- **Physical Exam:** Diffuse swelling, tense, tender posterior compartment - localizes the compartment syndrome to the posterior compartment of the leg. Weak distal pulses (but palpable) - indicates potential vascular compromise, common in severe compartment syndrome.
- **Neurological Exam:**
    - Intact sensation on medial foot - suggests the tibial nerve (innervates medial foot sensation) is relatively spared or less severely affected.
    - Absent sensation on lateral foot and dorsum of foot - points towards involvement of the common peroneal (fibular) nerve or its branches (superficial and deep peroneal nerves).
    - Weakness in plantarflexion and inversion - plantarflexion is primarily innervated by the tibial nerve (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris), and inversion is primarily by the posterior tibial artery (tibialis posterior, innervated by tibial nerve). This indicates significant tibial nerve involvement.
    - Normal dorsiflexion and eversion - dorsiflexion is innervated by the deep peroneal nerve (a branch of the common peroneal nerve), and eversion is innervated by the superficial peroneal nerve (a branch of the common peroneal nerve). This finding is somewhat contradictory to the sensory loss pattern, but the *weakness* pattern strongly points to tibial nerve dysfunction.
- **Ankle Reflexes:** Absent Achilles tendon reflex (S1 nerve root, mediated by the tibial nerve) - confirms significant tibial nerve dysfunction.
- **Procedure:** Fasciotomy of the posterior compartment - confirms the diagnosis of posterior compartment syndrome.
- **Question:** Which nerve is *most likely* compromised?

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Posterior Compartment Anatomy:** The posterior compartment of the leg contains the deep posterior compartment (containing the deep muscles: flexor hallucis longus, flexor digitorum longus, tibialis posterior) and the superficial posterior compartment (containing the superficial muscles: gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris).
- **Nerve Course:** The tibial nerve (L4-S3) enters the posterior compartment after passing through the popliteal fossa, deep to the popliteal artery and vein. It runs down the posterior aspect of the leg, deep to the fascia, between the heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. It then passes posterior to the medial malleolus, dividing into the medial calcaneal nerve (innervates flexor hallucis brevis, abductor hallucis, and skin on the medial heel) and the lateral plantar nerve (innervates most intrinsic foot muscles and skin on the lateral sole).
- **Vascular Supply:** The posterior tibial artery is the main artery supplying the posterior compartment.
- **Compartment Syndrome:** Increased pressure within a closed fascial compartment compromises circulation and nerve function. The posterior compartment is particularly vulnerable due to its tight fascial boundaries.
- **Tibial Nerve Function:** Motor: Plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris), Inversion (tibialis posterior), Toe flexion (flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus). Sensory: Medial aspect of the foot and sole. Reflex: Achilles tendon reflex.
- **Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve Function:** The common peroneal nerve branches off the sciatic nerve in the popliteal fossa. It winds around the fibular head, where it is vulnerable to injury. It then divides into the superficial peroneal nerve (innervates lateral compartment muscles - eversion; sensory to dorsum of foot and lateral leg) and the deep peroneal nerve (innervates anterior compartment muscles - dorsiflexion; sensory to web space between 1st and 2nd toes).
- **Sural Nerve Function:** Formed by branches of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. Sensory to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and lateral foot.
- **Saphenous Nerve Function:** A branch of the femoral nerve. Sensory to the medial aspect of the leg and foot.
- **Analysis:** The patient has signs of posterior compartment syndrome (pain, swelling, tenseness in the posterior compartment). The neurological findings are key: absent Achilles reflex, weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, and intact sensation on the medial foot strongly implicate the tibial nerve. The absent sensation on the lateral foot and dorsum is more typical of common peroneal nerve involvement, but the motor and reflex findings overwhelmingly point to the tibial nerve being the *most likely* nerve compromised within the posterior compartment syndrome. The tibial nerve runs deep within the posterior compartment and is highly susceptible to compression from swelling.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** This is the correct answer. The clinical presentation (posterior compartment syndrome) combined with the specific neurological deficits (absent Achilles reflex, weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, intact medial foot sensation) directly points to tibial nerve compression within the posterior compartment.
- (B) **Common peroneal (fibular) nerve:** This nerve is vulnerable in the popliteal fossa and around the fibular head, *not* typically within the posterior compartment itself. While the sensory loss pattern (lateral foot/dorsum) might suggest common peroneal involvement, the motor weakness (plantarflexion/inversion) and absent Achilles reflex are inconsistent with isolated common peroneal nerve injury. Common peroneal injury would cause foot drop (inability to dorsiflex) and loss of eversion.
- (C) **Sural nerve:** This nerve provides sensation to the posterolateral lower leg and lateral foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the pattern described (intact medial foot, absent lateral foot/dorsum sensation). It is not primarily a motor nerve and is not typically affected by posterior compartment syndrome.
- (D) **Saphenous nerve:** This nerve is a branch of the femoral nerve and provides sensation to the medial leg. It is not located in the posterior compartment and is not involved in plantarflexion or the Achilles reflex. Injury would cause sensory loss on the medial leg.
- (E) **Superficial peroneal (fibular) nerve:** This is a branch of the common peroneal nerve. It innervates the lateral compartment muscles (eversion) and provides sensation to the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg. Injury would cause weakness in eversion and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg. It is not located in the posterior compartment and does not innervate plantarflexors or mediate the Achilles reflex.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Tibial Nerve (Correct)                                  | Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve (Distractor B) | Sural Nerve (Distractor C) | Saphenous Nerve (Distractor D) | Superficial Peroneal Nerve (Distractor E) |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Location**         | Posterior compartment of leg, deep to fascia           | Popliteal fossa, around fibular head             | Posterolateral leg          | Medial leg                      | Lateral compartment of leg                |
| **Motor Function**   | Plantarflexion, Inversion, Toe Flexion                | Dorsiflexion (Deep branch), Eversion (Superficial branch) | None                       | None                            | Eversion                                  |
| **Sensory Function** | Medial foot, sole                                      | Dorsum of foot, lateral leg (Superficial branch), Web space 1st/2nd toe (Deep branch) | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot | Medial leg, medial foot        | Dorsum of foot, lateral leg              |
| **Reflex**           | Achilles (S1)                                          | None                                            | None                        | None                            | None                                      |
| **Compartment Syndrome Relevance** | Highly relevant in posterior compartment syndrome | Less relevant (injury usually higher up)       | Not relevant                | Not relevant                    | Not relevant                              |
| **Key Clinical Findings in Vignette** | Weak plantarflexion/inversion, absent Achilles reflex, intact medial foot sensation | Foot drop, loss of eversion, sensory loss on dorsum/lateral foot | Sensory loss posterolateral leg/lateral foot | Sensory loss medial leg/foot | Weak eversion, sensory loss dorsum/lateral foot |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Compartment syndrome can selectively compromise nerves within the affected compartment. In the posterior compartment, the tibial nerve is the most vulnerable major nerve, leading to weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, loss of sensation on the medial foot/sole, and an absent Achilles reflex. Differentiating tibial nerve injury from common peroneal nerve injury relies on understanding their respective motor functions (plantarflexion vs. dorsiflexion/eversion) and sensory distributions.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Lower Limb: Extended High-Yield Concept 146
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships and potential injuries involving the sciatic nerve and its branches (tibial and common peroneal/fibular nerves) within the popliteal fossa and distal lower leg, specifically relating to compartment syndromes and nerve entrapment. (理解坐骨神经及其分支（胫神经和腓神经）在腘窝和下腿远端部的解剖关系以及潜在损伤，特别是与深层筋膜室综合征和神经卡压有关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male marathon runner presents to the emergency department after collapsing during a race. He reports severe pain in his right lower leg that started abruptly during the race. On examination, his right leg is diffusely swollen, tense, and exquisitely tender to palpation, particularly in the posterior compartment. He has intact sensation on the dorsum of the foot but diminished sensation on the plantar surface. Motor examination reveals an inability to plantarflex the foot against resistance (foot drop is absent, but toe walking is impossible) and normal dorsiflexion and eversion strength. Distal pulses (dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial) are palpable, but capillary refill is delayed. An MRI of the right lower leg reveals diffuse edema and increased signal intensity within the posterior compartment muscles, with evidence of compression of a major nerve trunk within this compartment.

Which nerve is most likely compressed in this patient?

(A) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Tibial nerve
(D) Saphenous nerve
(E) Deep peroneal (fibular) nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Marathon runner collapsing during race:** Suggests exertional injury, potentially compartment syndrome due to muscle swelling.
- **Severe pain, swelling, tenseness, tenderness in the right lower leg, especially posterior compartment:** Classic signs of acute compartment syndrome, localized to the posterior compartment.
- **Intact sensation on dorsum of foot, diminished sensation on plantar surface:** The dorsum of the foot is primarily innervated by the superficial peroneal (fibular) nerve (lateral aspect) and the sural nerve (posterior aspect). The plantar surface is innervated by the tibial nerve (medial and lateral plantar nerves). This sensory pattern points to tibial nerve involvement.
- **Inability to plantarflex against resistance (foot drop absent, toe walking impossible):** Plantarflexion is primarily mediated by the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles, innervated by the tibial nerve. Dorsiflexion (tested as foot drop absence) is mediated by the anterior compartment muscles, innervated by the deep peroneal (fibular) nerve. Eversion is mediated by the lateral compartment muscles, innervated by the superficial peroneal (fibular) nerve. The inability to plantarflex confirms tibial nerve dysfunction.
- **Normal dorsiflexion and eversion strength:** Rules out significant injury to the deep and superficial peroneal (fibular) nerves, respectively.
- **Palpable distal pulses, delayed capillary refill:** Suggests vascular compromise is present but not complete occlusion, consistent with compartment syndrome potentially affecting both neurovascular structures.
- **MRI findings:** Confirms posterior compartment edema and nerve compression.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Posterior Compartment of the Leg:** Contains the superficial posterior compartment (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris) and the deep posterior compartment (tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus).
- **Tibial Nerve Course:** The tibial nerve is the terminal branch of the sciatic nerve. It descends through the popliteal fossa, posterior to the popliteal artery and vein, and enters the posterior compartment of the leg between the heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. It runs deep to the soleus muscle, accompanied by the posterior tibial artery and vein. Within the posterior compartment, it gives off branches to the muscles and then divides into the medial and lateral plantar nerves at the ankle.
- **Nerve Supply:**
    - Tibial Nerve: Innervates all muscles of the posterior compartment (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus) and provides sensory innervation to the plantar surface of the foot via its terminal branches (medial and lateral plantar nerves).
    - Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve: Branches off the sciatic nerve in the popliteal fossa. It wraps around the neck of the fibula and divides into the superficial and deep peroneal (fibular) nerves.
        - Superficial Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve: Innervates the lateral compartment muscles (fibularis longus, fibularis brevis) and provides sensation to the dorsum of the foot (except the first web space) and the lateral aspect of the lower leg.
        - Deep Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve: Innervates the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, fibularis tertius) and provides sensation to the first web space of the foot.
    - Sural Nerve: Formed by branches of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It runs superficially down the posterior aspect of the leg and provides sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot.
    - Saphenous Nerve: A branch of the femoral nerve. It runs down the medial aspect of the leg, providing sensation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot. It does not pass through the posterior compartment.
- **Compartment Syndrome:** Increased pressure within a closed fascial compartment compromises circulation and nerve function. The posterior compartment is a common site, especially after exertional injuries. The tibial nerve, running deep within this compartment, is particularly vulnerable to compression.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (posterior compartment pain/swelling, plantar sensory loss, inability to plantarflex) directly correlate with compression of the tibial nerve within the posterior compartment.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Common peroneal (fibular) nerve:** Injury typically occurs around the fibular neck. It would cause foot drop (weak dorsiflexion, inability to heel walk) and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg. This patient has normal dorsiflexion and sensation on the dorsum. Incorrect.
- (B) **Sural nerve:** Provides sensation to the posterolateral lower leg and lateral foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not plantar sensory loss or motor weakness related to plantarflexion. Incorrect.
- (D) **Saphenous nerve:** A branch of the femoral nerve, runs medially. Injury causes sensory loss on the medial lower leg and foot. It does not pass through the posterior compartment and is not involved in plantarflexion. Incorrect.
- (E) **Deep peroneal (fibular) nerve:** Innervates anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexors) and provides sensation to the first web space. Injury causes foot drop and sensory loss in the first web space. This patient has normal dorsiflexion. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Tibial Nerve (Correct)                                  | Common Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve | Sural Nerve                      | Saphenous Nerve                 | Deep Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :----------------------------- |
| **Location**            | Posterior compartment of leg, deep to soleus             | Popliteal fossa, wraps fibular neck | Posterior leg, superficial      | Medial leg, superficial        | Anterior compartment of leg    |
| **Motor Innervation**   | Posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors)          | Lateral & Anterior compartments | None                            | None                            | Anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexors) |
| **Sensory Innervation** | Plantar surface of foot                                 | Dorsum of foot (lateral), lateral leg | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot | Medial leg, medial foot        | 1st web space of foot          |
| **Clinical Scenario**   | Posterior compartment syndrome, sciatic nerve injury     | Fibular neck fracture, compartment syndrome | Superficial nerve injury        | Femoral nerve injury           | Anterior compartment syndrome, fibular neck injury |
| **Key Deficit**         | Plantarflexion weakness, plantar sensory loss            | Foot drop, dorsum sensory loss | Lateral foot sensory loss       | Medial foot sensory loss       | Foot drop, 1st web sensory loss |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The tibial nerve is vulnerable to compression within the posterior compartment of the leg, especially in cases of compartment syndrome. Clinical presentation involves weakness of plantarflexion and sensory loss on the plantar surface of the foot. Differentiating tibial nerve injury from peroneal nerve injuries relies on identifying the specific motor deficits (plantarflexion vs. dorsiflexion) and sensory distributions (plantar vs. dorsum of foot). Remember the course of the tibial nerve deep within the posterior compartment.
